So people that you think are rich, like a millionaire, if he's got two, three, four million dollars, that's not real wealth.

I mean, for us, obviously it is. But in terms of who's got money in this country, that's not real wealth. That's peanuts.

And even Bill Gates, they did a thing on Bill Gates. Bill Gates is still only worth about 30% of what Rockefeller is worth, for instance, right? So he's an upstart, you know, even though... So the point is that money is a doula, bain al-arniya, which one of the reasons why Allah did something in the Quran, which in terms of human history is extremely radical. The inheritance laws in Islam are designed to break monopolies.

You see, if you look at the Sharia, it's designed to spread the wealth out. In traditional cultures, the progenitor, the firstborn, all the money would go to the firstborn. And in English culture, people were out of their houses, right? I mean, if women didn't... If they didn't have a male protector, they lost their houses, which is one of Jane Austen's themes, right, in her stories of women just literally losing everything because there wasn't a husband.

I mean, she saw it as an injustice. Even though she's a comic writer, she saw it as a type of injustice to women. In fact, there's no male, and they lose everything the moment the father drops dead because some uncle or distant cousin who's a male gets to inherit all the money because the women don't inherit it.

In Islam, Allah broke that. So a woman would actually suddenly get money. Now, what did that do? If a woman marries outside of the family, it takes money outside of the family, which was one of the reasons why oftentimes wealthy families like to marry within the family to try to maintain it somewhat.

But you can see that in Islam. There really is an attempt to break down that type of monopoly in wealth, which once it occurs, you have a doula, what the Quran calls a doula, wealth only revolves around the rich people and that will cause a type of resentment, right? So in Islam, traditionally, there was always a very strong middle class. And where you have a strong middle class, you're going to have a lot less social pressure than where you have an extremely wealthy class of say, you know, maybe 10% with all the wealth and then 90% impoverished.

That creates a massive tension. Whereas if you have, you know, 40 to 60% in the middle somewhere, right, it enables one, the people down here, to be able to, you know, move into that middle class because it's not so far off. Whereas in this type situation, the gap is so great, it creates resentment and hassle.

And so poor people begin to envy the rich people. And then you're ripe for, you know, the communist manifesto and this type of thing. And look how many people died.

I mean, all those people that died, you know, millions of people because of communist revolution and it's all about resentment. And the other interesting thing about communism, nowhere is Marx critical of industrialization and massification. He never questions, you know, the whole technological society and where it was headed.

And Baldrallard wrote a really interesting book criticizing that aspect of communist critique that they never deal with, you know, factories. And, you know, he's all just saying, well, let's make the factories, the people working in them own the factories. But is factory life, do we really want people to be working like that and those, you know, that type thing? That's never questioned.

So that's a really important thing to reflect on, people, about this idea of resentment and a lot of what people actually think is righteous indignation is nothing other than Hasid, envy and resentment, really. Seen it a lot. And one of the things that you saw happen in the Muslim world is a lot of the communists, interestingly enough, in the 70s, in the 80s became Islamists, right? And changed all the vocabulary, right? And suddenly started talking about the social justice of Islam as opposed to the social justice of communism.

And so you just, Islam becomes a vehicle, again, to realize your own petty dunya aspirations, right? People have to be really wary of that type of Islam being used as a vehicle for anger that is not rooted in anger for the sake of Allah. It's rooted in anger simply because one is dissatisfied with one's worldly position, right? And that's a really tricky one to tell you. And then he says also, He says, also think about this.

It doesn't benefit your one iota to have envy, nor does it cause the one you're envying. He's not going to lose that niqmah because you're envious of it. Allah gave it to him, Allah will take it away.

It's not you that's going to do anything. So it's really a waste of time. Now he goes into the asbab, which is really important here because the asbab, if you understand the causes, you can understand, you know, one, whether you've got it and two, how to get rid of it.

The first thing he mentions is adawa. Somebody doesn't, it's your enemy. And so like America, there's a lot of places that America is the enemy, so hassad, right? It becomes hassad.

And the really interesting thing about all these, a lot of these anti-American people, they're obsessed with American culture, right? So the guy hijacking the plane has Nike tennis shoes and he's got his t-shirt with Mickey Mouse on it and, you know, orders hamburgers and milkshakes. I don't know, but I mean, you see it. American culture is just everywhere.

It's just rapidly globalizing, right? Now, why is it that people in Beijing go to a McDonald's? Why? I don't understand, but they do. And so, you know, the fact that a lot of people are anti-American, again, one has to question, what are they anti-American about? Is it because they're just envious of the fact that America happens to be the dunyui most powerful country on the earth? Americans have the dunyui highest standard of living, blah, blah, blah, whatever it is. Is that the reason? Or is it genuinely do they see that this is unfair? You know, this is an oppressive society that's doing harm.

So, again, people have to be really careful about what their intentions are and where they are. So, Adawa is a cause of hassad. You might not like somebody that you're working with in the office, so when good things happen to him, you get angry.

You get, there's hassad. You can just cause Adawa. Very common.

Another one, tahubub. Tahubub is a type of hassad. There's almost an element of humor in it, but it can get quite vicious.

And tahubub relates to the object. You have two people or more, and they all have the same object of desire, like parents, children, siblings, are fighting for parental love. Mother only has a limited amount of time in the day.

She's got five children. So, those children are vying for her attention. And there's a book, although it's written by, I think, Solomon called Born to Rebel, which is all about birth orders.

He did this massive statistical study about birth order, and he's convinced that people's personalities are a direct result of all this envy they had in trying to get their parents. And with massive sweeping theories, you always have to be careful, but obviously there's an element of truth in that. So, birth order is very interesting, because first-borns tend to be much less rebellious, according to his research, than later-borns.

In fact, most of the real rebellious type people in the history, in social history, have been later-borns, not first-borns. And he said it has to do with the fact that they see the first-born, he's got all the perks, and so they want to rebel against that kind of status quo. And first-borns historically tend to be the ones that are always supporting the status quo and don't want.

It's an interesting study. So, that's the hubbub. Another one is a teacher.

People, students that vie for the teachers, like teacher's pets type people, that want, and they have this envy. Another one is, it's called the da'udarayr, the disease of second wives, right? Hasr. Because, you know, if there's more than one wife in the situation, obviously there's one husband.

Husbands' times tend to be limited, and so they're vying. And that's why sharia is so adamant about being fair to the wife. If you give one to one, you have to give one to the other.

Have to. That is a given. And the knights are the same, I think, for that reason.

Now, obviously, the word in Arabic for second wife is dara, sometimes mispronounced dura, but it's actually a dara. Dara means harm, right? That's what it means. It means harm.

So, obviously, I don't know if the first wife named the second wife dara or not, but it indicates that the Arabs understood something about the psychology of multiple marriages and that the second wife will bring in a type of dara to the first wife. So, that does happen. And you can get that vying for things.

So, that's tahabur. And then takabur is arrogance. That causes envy.

If you see somebody getting ahead of you and you're arrogant, you see, he doesn't deserve that. I'm better than him. That's takabur.

He should have got that promotion. And this is something that some of the Arabs had in, like al-Waleed ibn al-Mukhayrah, Abu Jahal, you know. They had that against the Prophet SallAllahu Alaihi Wasallam.

They thought themselves, he's Yateem al-Bani Hashem. He's the orphan of Bani Hashem. He's Ibn al-Abi Qabshah.

That's what they called him. Abi Qabshah is his father by wet nursing. So, they just saw, he doesn't deserve this ni'ana of being spoken to from the heavens.

We deserve it more than he does. So, that's takabur, which is, I think, pretty obvious. Ta'azuz is less so than takabur.

Ta'azuz has to do with a person wanting the status quo. In other words, he doesn't mind feeling the same as the other person. As long as we have the same things, it's okay.

But suddenly, you get the job promotion, and I didn't. And then, my question is, I start saying, he's going to start looking down on me. Right? So, the dynamic changes.

So, Ta'azuz is, I'm trying to maintain my izah. So, really, it's related to a poor self-esteem, and I think that's how I translate it. Poor self-worth.

Ta'azuz is an attempt to re-establish an equilibrium, because you're feeling that your self-worth is compromised by the fact that another person has more than you do. And so, that's also a cause of hasad. And you can see that also in the first Muslims who, when they, for instance, they said, lola nuzirahad al quran al-rajirim.

Right? Minar qariyatani al-im. Had this quran only been revealed to a man from one of the two big cities who was great, a great man. In other words, if it would have been revealed to Walid ibn Mureyra, we wouldn't mind bowing our heads to him and admitting that, you know, you're the prophet.

But this is Yateen Beni Hashem. How can he be over us? In other words, we want him to stay the same as us. So, this is something that happened, afflicted those first Arabs.

And also those who said, this is just hada basharu, mithilana. Right? This is just a man like us. How can he be a prophet and we're not prophets? In other words, we don't like this idea of somebody who's a common man.

If it's a king, that's all right. We can deal with that because the king's better than us. We know that.

But he's my next door neighbor. Right? He shouldn't have anything better than me. So, it's that idea there.

And also, you find that Faraon says, actually that's more takabur. But he says about Musa, you know, that how can a prophet come and his people are our slaves, you know, looking down on them. So, these are all reasons why people reject the truth.

And Ta'ajob has to do with, you just can't believe it. It's more, you know, I can't believe. It's not takabur.

It's not ta'azos. It's just type of, you know, this can't be happening. Right? This person couldn't have won the jackpot.

I've known him all my life. This can't be happening. So, it's a hasa that's really related to a type of disbelief, you know, in somebody getting a blessing that you just can't believe that he has that blessing.

It doesn't make any sense. And then also, hubrriyasa, love of leadership, always wanting to maintain one's position of authority. Sometimes hubrriyasa will lead a person to not want somebody to get a blessing just so they can maintain the position over them of authority, which is similar to takabur, but different in a sense because he does have a position of leadership or he desires to have it over that person and by them getting some type of blessing, it's going to lead to changing that equilibrium or creating a disequilibrium there.

So, and then shuhunhati, shuh is covetousness. So, that's also a cause of envy, just pure shuh. Like you see somebody has something, you just have a shahiya, you're covetous of it, you want it.

And that's a big disease. Which Allah says, waminyukashuhanapsifa wurekumunu fahbihun, those who are saved from their shuh. Now, another thing, one of the concepts that Imam al-Ghazadi mentions often is because these diseases, in fact, are part of our nature, the process is to transform them and make them something beneficial to you.

For instance, Native Americans, some of the Native American tribes in this country used to name their children names that had disabilities, right? Like lame deer. They would give it a name that had a disability. And the reason for that was that they noticed that animals that had disabilities tended to overcompensate.

And it would make them more clever than the other. They'd have to be more aware. And so there's an idea of getting your disability to become an advantage.

And this is what really successful people in dunyawy terms tend to do. And what Imam al-Ghazadi is saying is, this is what the ukhrawi people need to do with these disadvantages aspects of their selves, to turn them into advantages. So, for instance, the Prophet SallAllahu Alaihi Wasallam said, la hasada il lafiyat an attain.

There's no envy except in two people, Rajulin, Utsiya, Malin. He was given wealth for sallbatahu alal khayri. He used his wealth for good.

Right? And in the rihwah, it says that, hiyun fiqhul an alayluat raffan nahar. You know, he's giving it out by night and by day. So that's a good reason to envy that person.

Not that you want him to lose the envy, to lose the nama, but that you actually want to be like him to have wealth so that you can also give out. And that is called in Arabic, rhipa, which is different from hasad. Rhipa is good envy.

It's a positive envy. You're envying the person. You don't want them to lose their nama.

You want them to keep their nama. But you wish that you had what they had. And the other person is, Rajulin, Utsiya, hikma.

He was given hikma, wisdom, fayu alimu hanaas. So he teaches that wisdom to people. And so those two people, in other words, what we're being told is, don't envy people over petty, trivial things like dunya.

It's not worth it. Envy people for things that are high things, like akhira. And then what that will do is it will make you set out to achieve what they've achieved.

All right? So... And then he says about... Those are the causes. And then he says... As for a blessing that a kafir has, a non-Muslim or a bad Muslim, fajr. Somebody who's fajr.

They have fujoor. A profligate. Somebody who does kabaya.

A fajr or somebody who does kabaya openly. As for a fajr, he says... The disease of second wives is permissible in this case. I'm going to use the second wife because it works with the rhyme.

And also I think it indicates that that's, you know, it's a natural type of hassad. You know, it's a good thing to have hassad towards non-Muslims and... But obviously that has to be because they're using it for bad things. Do you see the difference? In other words, what I was saying earlier about having envy towards non-Muslims just because they have dunya and you don't.

That's not a good reason. The reason that he's saying it's a good thing to do is because they have dunya that they're using for harm. They're harming people with it and they're using it to oppress.

And in that case it's justifiable. It's permissible. And he says... And then he says... Blameworthy modesty.

Modesty is a good thing. It's virtuous. Al-hayyāʿu min an-īmān.

The hadī says al-hayyāʿu min an-īmān. And hayā is from imān. So this hayā, which is called al-hayyāʿu, the mean, blameworthy.

This is... The blameworthy modesty is a modesty that prevents you from making inkār of mūnkār when you see it. So you see somebody doing something wrong and you really should say something, but you're too shy. You're too shy.

Like a child... An adult child with the parents. There's a type of modesty because they're my parents. I can't say anything to them.

A mūnkār is a mūnkār, whether it's the parents or anybody else, if it genuinely is a mūnkār. Now, just to say a few things about mūnkār. There's a condition about mūnkār.

One, a mūnkār has to be mūjmaʿalei, in order to make inkār of it. It has to be agreed upon. You can't make mūnkār of something that's called mukterefihi, which means there's difference of opinion about it.

So whenever there's difference of opinion, you have to be very careful about mūnkār, because technically the mūnkār of one scholar might not be the mūnkār of another scholar. And unless it's absolutely agreed upon that it's a mūnkār, then you can't make inkār of it. And they said about Imam al-Maziri, he was so knowledgeable that he rarely made inkār of anybody, because he could always find a dealil for, a proof for their action somewhere.

And that's a really important thing to know now, just because there's so much, Muslims make so much inkār of each other. There's this impulse now, just everybody attacks everybody else. So, blameworthy modesty is something where you don't make inkār where you should, or to ask a question about the deen.

That's blameworthy modesty, and that's why I say that to Aisha al-Dilam has said that the best women were the women of the almsar, because modesty did not prevent them from learning the deen. So she was praising them, saying that even though there's things that people would be modest about, it didn't stop them. Like the Prophet SallAllahu Alaihi Wasallam, who somebody came to him, a woman asking about the period, and he was explaining to her, and she kept asking, and finally he just asked Aisha to show her what to do, because it was embarrassing for him.

And Aisha could answer the question, because she was pakīhā. But the point is that that woman wanted to know, and even some of the women, they used to send in a little box the cloth from their period to ask the hukam from Aisha about the color. That was like asking to make sure they understood when the hayith ended and when it started.

So that's obviously most women, they would not feel comfortable with that at all, because they're modest about it. But the point is that those women were so bent on doing what Allah or so straight about doing what Allah told them to do that their modesty didn't prevent them from doing those things. And that would tend to be more with women than with men, but it applies to both, because there are some men that are extremely modest, so they don't ask questions.

And then he said, Amma hayah ukharimin kamājara, lirmus pafa is zaynaban takam mara. As for the hayah of generosity, just as it occurred with muspafas allāh islam when he married zaynab, takam mara means, you know, to spend the night zaynab, the first night of their marriage. What happened was the Prophet said, He said, Marry zaynab rādīla anha.

And he invited people over for a walima, a wedding ceremony, and they came, and then they didn't leave. Three of them stayed behind, and the Prophet, being his generous he was, he sat with them, but he just got married, and it's his wedding night, right? And so he's waiting for them to leave, and they want to be with the Prophet SallAllahu Alaihi Wasallam. They're enjoying the company, and so they didn't leave.

And so he got up, left the room to kind of give them the idea, and then he came back, and they were still there, so he sat down, and then he finally left again. And then the ayah revealed, you know, to leave the Prophet when you finished your business, to leave, you know, to go, and they thought, I'm such a good old, when you finish eating, go, move along. And so that type of modesty is not blameworthy, because there's no haukam sharai, and it's out of his karam that he didn't say, you know, it's my wedding night, can you please go now? It was out of his karam, SallAllahu Alaihi Wasallam, that he didn't say that.

I mean, there would be some people that would, you know, they wouldn't have any problem with that at all. But he was so generous that it wasn't in his nature to do that, and so he's saying that is not a, that's not a blameworthy. Wa ashwah ar-qallamun al-waleemati, he fed them, wa kharaju an-hussi wa tharathati, and they left except for three.

Labu wala mi'atmor bi'il pe'lakhi, fa'u wamun mahasun al-akhlaki. So they stayed, and he didn't tell them to leave, and so that's from good character. That's not blameworthy.

And then he said, lokana rajlan, that's for the line of poetry, kana rajlan swariha, and it's a way of saying rajul. Had, and it comes from a hadith, lokana rahaya o rajulan, lakana rajulan swarihan. If, if modesty had been a man, he would have been a righteous man.

So it's, it's a hadith of the Prophet explaining that modesty is, is a very, very high thing. Now he's talking about the heart's engagement in matters that do not concern it. So he's talking about ar-khold.

Khold al-khald is the movement of the heart, and basically the heart, he'll, he'll go into it later about, I think it's, I hope we get to that. I think we'll get to it. But he goes into great detail about the, the types of thoughts and things like that.

And so what he's saying here is that, Khold, if you have the heart moving or reflecting in things that are prohibited, then it's prohibited. But that is also, it's what, it's what they call tafakkur, or takhaion, not tafakkur, takhaion. Tafakkur is when you think about maqulat and takhaol is when you think about mahsuzat, sensory things.

So he says there that, al-kholdu hima raisa yaaniinama yahram wahaythukana fima harama, if you're thinking about something that is haram, then it's haram to think about that thing. And then he gives you an example, kebfikri hima hasan al-ajanavi, like reflecting on the beauty, fantasizing about the beautiful qualities of those unrelated to one by marriage, from male or female. So thinking about things that one shouldn't be thinking about is considered prohibited.

And also, including that would be weaknesses or faults of Muslims, whether they are absent or present. So actually reflecting on the faults of Muslims. Right? And you shouldn't even be doing that.

And that's why there's a hadiqubalim inshagarahu agua an'ayyubiraylihi. There's a tree in paradise for the one who's preoccupied with his own faults over the faults of others. His own preoccupation causes him to forget the faults of others.

And the older you get, the more serious you should take that. You know, really. And it's just life's too short to be.

Everybody's got faults. And if you start thinking about other people's faults, you're just a fool. The point is, is to get rid of your own.

And also, I put in there, because it's important, but it's in the commentary, also reflecting on the nature of Allah's essence. The that of Allah is haram. You can't think about the that of Allah, Subhanahu wa ta'ala.

So, fear of poverty is one of the diseases of the heart and Shaitan in the Quran, Allah tells us that he promises you, ya idukum al-fakari, he promises you that you're going to lose your money. That's something that Shaitan does. He promises you that you're going to lose your wealth.

And so, fear, and this is going on right now, this is a time of real khof al-fakar, because there's all this uncertainty in the dunya right now. So, fear of poverty is a bad opinion of Allah, because Allah promised to provide for you. Whoever has taqwa of Allah, Allah promised, that's a Jumla shartiya. In Arabic language, that's called a conditional sentence.

If you do this, I'll do this. And the condition is, if you have taqwa, I'll provide for you. That's a promise from Allah, Subh'anaHu Wa Ta-A'la.

If you have taqwa, I'll provide for you, and I'll do it from even where you wouldn't think it was coming. In other words, it won't be from the things that you wear, you expect it, your job, your monthly salary, whatever. Allah can provide you from places that you would never expect, but it's on the condition of taqwa.

If you don't have taqwa, then Allah is not obligated to take care of you. If you're not doing what He asked of you as a condition for walking on the earth with dignity, because if you don't do what He said, you're going to walk on the earth in humiliation. Especially the Muslims, because we should always remember that the dunya is the paradise of the non-Muslims.

So if they have their good now, you should be, you know, this is all they're getting. You know, this is all they're getting. Let them enjoy it.

Seriously, think about that. This is all they're getting. And this is what, there are different people it's related to.

I've seen it related to two different people. But the meaning of the story is sound anyway. There was a Jewish man who was an oil, he used to sell oil.

And he would heat up oil as well and sell hot oil. And he was covered in the oil and his clothes were dirty. And he was a poor man in Cairo.

And one day this item, very famous, wealthy item came out. He had a white horse came out. And this Jewish man came out to the horse and said to him, do you relate a hadith that says that this world is the paradise of the disbeliever and the prison of the believer? And he said yes.

And he said, then I have a question for you. What kind of paradise am I in? And what kind of prison are you in? And so they say he looked up and then he looked down. And then he said to him, when I contemplate what is waiting for you because of your disbelief in the next world, I can only assume that you're in paradise now.

And when I contemplate on what Allah has prepared for me, for my belief in the next world, I can only see this as a prison. And as the story goes, he said, a shadow in Allah. But the point is, this is all relative.

Poverty is a relative state. And your fear of poverty, it's also a foolish thing because what benefit is it? American humorist Mark Twain said, most of my problems I never actually saw them. Right? In other words, you've got all these concerns that you think about and you never actually see them.

They're just in your head. And so that's part of the thing about having fear of poverty. It just ends up in your head.

And if you're impoverished, you don't have any fear of it. Right? What are you afraid of? That's your state. But if you have money and you're always afraid of poverty, what's the point of having money? If it's going to create all this anxiety for you and all this problem and all this trouble, you're better off without it.

And Al-Harmun, Al-Harmun, anxiety is half of growing old. It's a hadith. Anxiety is half of aging.

And there's a hadith, Al-Biru, Yezidu, Al-Ammar. Righteousness will increase your age. It'll lengthen your life.

It's a hadith. And one of the interpretations of that that I think is really interesting is people who are righteous don't have anxiety. And people who are irreligious, they're in this constant state of turmoil within themselves.

And so their hearts are never at ease and they have all this anxiety, so they age faster than other people. So their life gets worn out. They dissipate quicker than other people.

So fear of poverty is a waste of time. And now he says the cure for it is to have a good opinion of Allah. An-An-An-An-An-An-An-An-An-An.

Right? Allah Subh'anaHu Wa Ta-A'la says, I don't want them to feed me and I don't want them to provide for me. Right? Allah says that. I don't expect that from you.

And then he says that he is the razaq, Dhul-Qulit al-Mateen. Right? Allah is the razaq. I'm the one who razaq is a mubarakah.

It's an exaggerated form, always providing, continuously providing. So Allah is the provider, have a good opinion of him. And then he says to know also, and know that Allah is not diminished.

His mulk, his dominion is not diminished by him giving out. If he's giving somebody all this wealth over here, it doesn't diminish his mulk. And that's why you shouldn't have hassads for him.

Because just like he made him wealthy, he can make you wealthy. But again, you should be content. That's why the Prophet SallAllahu Alaihi Wasallam said, Al-Qana'atuh, Kansun La'atna.

Contentment is a treasure that's never exhausted. So know that what he said. And then also know, The thing that's been allotted for you, it will reach you.

Now the other thing is Surat al-Ikhlas. Ikhlas, if you look at the actual meaning of the word, Ikhlas comes from a word khalousah, which means to be pure. And it relates to honey.

Pure honey, leaven, khalis, pure milk. Leaven, khalis is leaven that has no, nothing's come into it. It's pure, right out of the udder.

So it hasn't been contaminated by anything. And that's what the deen al-khalis to Allah is that which is not contaminated with riyyah. That you worship Allah and there's no contamination of riyyah, of hidden shirk, of doing it for other than Allah's sake.

And the mukhli seen are the people that do that. Now if you look in the Qur'an, there's two words that are used. Mukhlas and mukhlis.

You can find them both in the Qur'an. The mukhlis is the active participle and the mukhlas is the passive, which indicate there has to be a tawfiq from Allah. It's not enough for you to be sincere from yourself.

There has to be a tawfiq from Allah subhanahu wa ta'ala as well. And that's the secret of success, is that Allah gives this tawfiq. And that's why Abrahassan al-Shadri said, Allahumma ijaaal saiyati saiyat al-mahbul ladeik.

Walat ijaaal hasanati hasanat al-mabrodi ladeik. Make my bad actions the bad actions of those you love and don't make my good actions the good actions of those you hate. In other words, you have to have tawfiq from Allah.

And Allah promises tawfiq if you do certain things. So it goes together. And this is why if you're a mukhlas, but you're a muftedek, you're not mukhlas.

And Sahar ibn Abdila, great, great early scholar, Muhadith, amongst other things, said, manahab banyakari anas, adamaa bainahu wa bainarabihi, fa hua ghati loon. Whoever desires people to know what's between him and his Lord, he's in ghafla.

In other words, it's nobody's business, but you and your Lord. You shouldn't desire for other people to know. Unless he sees that people have left good actions and he does it as a way of nasi'a.

So, for instance, ibn ulema radilano and ibn misa'ud were known to go into the masjid before fajr and do to hajjud. So they did that. They could have done it in their houses, but they did it there as a tariqib for other people from the tabu'in to see them doing that so that they could do that and that wasn't their intention other than as a nasi'a to the Muslims.

Now, he says, when nizhmu lem yara bihi min baasi in bhuni asa'i wa ala asasi, ima madik radilano who's called a nizhim. And the reason he's called a nizhim is because his student and later to become mushtahid muktak, ima mushtahri radilano said, ida adukir al-urama fa madikun a nizhim. If we talk about that, ule madik is the star.

And that was something that his student said about him. And so he became known as a nizhim. It's one of the names of ima madik.

He has alqab, alimul madina, like Imam Shafir, he's called the Alimo Quraish. He's called the Alimo Madina. Abu Hanifa radilano is called often Al Imam al-Ahram, the greatest ima.

So those are alqab that are given to them. So he did not consider that harmful as long as the original intention was based on the foundation of sincerity. And this comes because Imam Madik radilano was once asked about a man, he said, what do you say about a man who's walking to the masjid for the sake of Allah? He went out with the sake of Allah.

I want to pray in the masjid, jama'ah. But on the way he says, he starts thinking, I hope somebody sees me going to the masjid. And he said, la'arra, la'arra, ba'asan, right? Iva kana alsarawil ikhlas.

He doesn't see that as harmful if he started out with ikhlas and fights it. In other words, he doesn't, you know, he doesn't follow through with that inner type, waswasa. But rather he actually says, awudu billa or something like that.

So that's important to know. And then he says, wa'amirun ala'riya an'afballu min tarkihi lekhawihi wa'fatballu dikar al-lisani farrag al-janani aragufur al-qalbi wa lisani. This is really important here.

What he's saying is, is that it's actually, to not do something out of fear of riya is worse than doing it with riya. So to leave an action, say you're coming into the masjid and you want to do some rakats and then you think, those people might think I'm doing it, or you're worried like, oh, you know, I'm probably just doing this because I want them to think that I'm a good Muslim. If that type of fear of riya, that's called khawfar riya.

If that occurs to the heart and you leave the action because of it, it's riya. Because the problem is, is the reason you left the action was the people. So the whole point is, you're concerned about the people.

So leaving it out of fear is the same as doing it for the people. You're concerned as the people, not Allah. And that's why they say it's better to do the action even if you feel there's a riya in it.

Now this does not mean if you have a possibility to do it on your own. In other words, if it's an ibada or a worship that's related to something you can hide from the people, then it's better to hide it. It's always better to do something secretly as opposed to publicly.

Why? Because of the danger of riya. But if you leave it out of fear of riya, it's worse than actually doing it with the riya. And this is also the same with dhikr.

That if it, according to the unama, it's better to do dhikr, heedless, than it is to not do dhikr at all. The unama say, it's better that your tongue is doing dhikr and your heart's rafid than that both your tongue and your heart are rafid. Because what they say is, if you do it on your tongue, it might reach the heart.

Because a man's heart, the heart of a man lies under his tongue. And it's interesting, in Chinese medicine, just in their physiology, there's a direct connection from the tongue to the heart. And they say that what the tongue does affects the heart, that it's actually even the movement of the tongue affects the heart.

And that's in their physiology. Now another thing is important also to know that riya, the derivation of riya actually comes from seeing a man, seeing the creation, not the opposite. In other words, you would think that riya is them seeing you.

But it's actually you seeing them. That's where the riya is. It's your seeing them as opposed to seeing Allah Subh'anaHu Wa Ta-A'la.

And one of the things about the Yoma Qiyama that's fascinating, one of the many things, is that Aisha Radhila, I know when she was told by the Prophet that everybody was naked, and she asked the Prophet, won't they be looking at each other? He said, it's going to be much greater than that, Aisha. In other words, people will be in such a state of awe, they won't even be aware of other people. And this is the point of ibada for the sake of Allah.

If it's really for the sake of Allah, then you have a knowledge of Allah that completely makes everybody else seem insignificant. How can you give, I mean when you think of Allah, what you're worshiping, I mean just imagine that the Prophet SallAllahu Alaihi Wasallam once saw Jibriel in his true form. He filled the entire horizon.

One angel. The whole horizon, that whole plane, you look out there and imagine seeing a being filling that whole horizon. It's unbelievable.

And that's Jibriel. So the point being is when you realize what you're worshiping is infinite, and then you start thinking about the pathetic, other pathetic human beings, not just yourself, but all these other pathetic creatures who are in the same mess that you're in. In other words, they need Allah as bad as you do.

There's nothing they can do for you. There's no benefit, no harm, nothing. And that's the bottom line.

And that's why he says, So now he's telling you why. Fear of other than Allah and desiring them, it obviates, it's against tawakkul. In other words, if you have true tawakkul, Allah, Allah Subh'anaHu Wa Ta-A'la says, well, we need tawakkul, Allah.

He's going to have tawakkul, Allah. What he's saying is, if you're worrying about other people, you don't have tawakkul, Allah. There's no tiklan.

And if you don't have tawakkul, Allah, the reason that you don't have it is because there's no Yaqeen. And what you don't have Yaqeen, the Yaqeen that you don't have is that everything good comes from Allah and everything harmful can only happen by the will of Allah. That's the Yaqeen that Tawheed is teaching us.

He's telling us to be absolutely certain that only Allah can harm you or benefit you and for that reason, you lose your fear of human beings. Which does not mean that you become like a fool and go start doing impetuous things without thinking, like driving like a madman or, well, it's all written by Allah. That is not tawakkul.

The Prophet SallAllahu Alaihi Wasallam said to the Bedouin about the camel, tie it and then have tawakkul, Allah. So then he says that he seeks refuge in Allah but the reason for this, every disease is lack of certainty. What is prohibited from the two is that which prevents an obligation from being fulfilled.

If on the other hand, it leads to neglecting that which is recommended, then it is considered reprehensible. In any case, flee in fear to your Lord from both of them. So don't desire creation and don't fear creation.

But what he's saying is, what is haram are those things that are going to prevent you from doing an obligation or it's maqrou that prevents you from doing something that's mustahab. So fear and desire from other than Allah Subh'anaHu Wa Ta-A'la. Now then he says, keep in mind that the cure for both is to know that there's no harm that benefits or harms other than him from Allah.

And then he goes now to the next which is called displeasure with the divine decree. And this is going to sound very familiar for a lot of people. This pleasure with the divine decree occurs when one resists Allah Subh'anaHu Wa Ta-A'la, the majestic exalted in what he has decreed.

For instance saying, I didn't warrant this happening to me. In other words, I don't deserve this. And that's really interesting because you hear that from a lot of people.

I don't deserve this. Why is this happening to me? Or the next one he gives you, what did I do to deserve this suffering? What wrong did I do to deserve this? So the point here is everything comes from Allah Subh'anaHu Wa Ta-A'la. And this is why Imam Abrahassan, said that there's a quality that people have that they're completely unaware of and it destroys all of their actions.

And he said very few people actually recognize this and it is being displeased with the divine decree. Very few people actually recognize that they're showing displeasure to the divine decree. And this is because Allah Subh'anaHu Wa Ta-A'la says they were displeased with what Allah has given to them.

And so Allah removed all of their good actions. Because the people of Taqwa, if they're asked what did your Lord bring down? They say good, it's all good. That's what they say, it's all good.

The moment everything is good, nothing is evil, it's all good. There's a reason, there's a lesson to be learned, a lot, there's a wisdom. And this is the difference between the people of Allah and the people other than Allah.

The people of Allah recognize that the world is a place of purification. It is a mapara itself. That we were sent here to purify ourselves.

The reason that we exist is to purify ourselves to draw near to Allah and worship Allah Subh'anaHu Wa Ta-A'la. That's it. Once you recognize that, then everything becomes an opportunity to learn and to increase and to draw near to Allah Subh'anaHu Wa Ta-A'la.

Everything. Because there's only four possible states of the human being. There's only four, that's it.

There's no fifth, guaranteed. And if you can come up with a fifth one, I'll be amazed. According to Abbas and Mursi, he said you're either going to be in Nirmah or you're going to be in Baliyah or you're going to be in Fara or you're going to be in Maasihah.

That's it. You're either in a blessing from Allah or a tribulation from Allah, obedience to Allah or disobedience. That's it.

Now, each one of these necessitates a response from Allah. Allah wants a response from you in each one of these. If Allah gives you a Nirmah, what's his response that he desires from us? Shukar.

He wants shukar. That is the response, gratitude. Now, what is gratitude according to Sharia? It is to use the Nirmah in what is acceptable to Allah.

Either mobah, permissible, mandu or wajib. To use it in something that is makru, there's not really gratitude there, but it's not ingratitude because it's not in the haram. That's what Allah is proving to you.

You look at those five things, there's only one that you're punishable by. The other four, you either get a reward or nothing. So shukar.

He says later on in the matara, that shukar is to utilize what Allah has given you in what is pleasing to Allah. That's shukar. So you have shukar al-lisan, you have shukar al-janan, al-lisan, al-banan.

You have shukar on the tongue and part of it is to thank the people. That's part of shukar. To tell other people what they did for you is part of shukar.

That's actually considered part of shukar. To remind other people, oh, so-and-so, he benefited me in this or that. Ihara ni'ma is part of shukar, to actually show the blessing.

Allah loves that. To feel the blessing in your heart and to act in something that's pleasing to Allah with the blessings. The next one is a tribulation, baliya.

And what does Allah expect from us? Sabar. Sabar. Sabar.

Allah expects, if he gives you a tribulation, he expects Sabarun Jamil. I mean, actually, you don't even have to have Sabarun Jamil. Sabarun Jamil is when you don't complain to anybody.

You can actually complain, and we'll get into it in a second, but that's not a good thing to tell people. It just makes you feel a little bit better. And then the next, fa'a, obedience, what is the response? Shuhud al-Minna.

The response is not to become arrogant. If you're in obedience, to recognize that it's from the tawfiq of Allah that you're in obedience. So you don't start thinking you're better than other people because you're in fa'a and they're in masiya.

That's the danger of obedience. And this is the danger of religion. The inherent danger of religion is self-righteousness.

And this is really the disease of the religious man. He begins to see himself as better than the irreligious man. And that's Shaitan's inroad.

Once he's got you in that corner, it's rope-a-dope, like Muhammad Ali did. It's rope-a-dope. He's got you till the end of the fight.

Because kibir is his game. That's Shaitan. Once he's got you in mutakabir, to takabir al-Anas, he's got you.

So that's the danger of obedience. What Allah expects you when you're obedient is to recognize it's a gift from Allah. And so you recognize that just as he's made you obedient, he could have made that person over there who's disobeying him, he could have given you the nama.

And the Prophet SallAllahu Alaihi Wasallam said, don't find faults in others. Because if you find faults in them, Allah will take their faults away from them and give them to you. So there should be a fear.

There should be a fear in recognizing that that person is in baliya. He's in baliya. And he has to change.

If he's in mausiya, it's a baliya as well. Because pa'a is a nama. So they go together.

But he's in a baliya. And this is why Imam Matic relates in the Mu'ata that Isa Alayhi Sallam said, according to Imam Matic, balarani an-na Isa bin imariam qal, which means I just heard. So it's a story.

But he obviously thought it was good enough to report it in his Mu'ata. He said that Isa Alayhi Sallam was reported to have said that there's only two types of people. There's people who are in tribulation and people who are in nama.

And he said, so don't look at the people of tribulation as if you're lords. But rather, look at them with compassion like servants would look at them. So that is the point.

When you see people who are in tribulation, don't look at them like I'm better than these people. Look at them like SubhanAllah, they're in tribulation and alhamdulillah al-diyafani. That's the du'a of the Prophet.

When he saw people in tribulation, he said alhamdulillah al-diyafani. Praise be to the one who made me healthy in a way that I wasn't given the tribulation of these people. Alhamdulillah alhamdulillah alhamdulillah alhamdulillah alhamdulillah.

He over so much of his creation. So that is where you should be with obedience. Now in Maasiyyah, obviously, what is expected of you? Tobah.

You have to make Tobah. Now, if you look at this situation and realize SubhanAllah, every single thing that happens to me is going to be one of these four things. And there's a response that I can do that will please Allah Subh'anaHu Wa Ta-A'la.

And if I please Allah, it means that that thing in fact was an amma. Because it took me closer to Allah and that's the point. Maybe you hate a thing and in it is much good for you.

And maybe you love a thing and in it is much harm for you. And Allah knows and you don't know. So just admit you don't know.

And then when it comes to Alhamdulillah, alhamdulillah, alhamdulillah, right? He ni'am. And even Abbas said if you get a tribulation, it's a ni'amma that's not worse than it could be. So you have to thank Allah.

Alhamdulillah, it's not that bad, right? And the best thing is to look at the Seerah of the Messenger of Allah, SallAllahu Alaihi Wasallam and see how he lived his life and how nobody had greater tribulation. He buried all of his children except for Fatima. How many people will do that in their life? How many people will bury all of their children except one? Out of six children, you bury five of them? He lost the closest people to him.

His most beloved uncle slaughtered on the battlefield. The beloved wife of several years of blissful marriage dies in his most difficult year. Abu Talib, his uncle, he was raised an orphan.

He had no supporters at one time. People who honored him were calling him a madman saying he was Majnun. I mean, how many people have that? Where suddenly all of your friends and coworkers and everything start calling you a Majnun, spitting on you.

Seriously, so you look, he had sanctions against him that most people couldn't bear, you know, anything near just that one period of his life. So all of those tribulations, and then you realize, never once, we have no... No complaint, no complaint. Never once.

Twenty-three years, no complaints. Alhamdulillah. That's all I knew, Alhamdulillah.

So this is the situation. So to be angry at the Qadr is to be in a state of ghafla, in a state of heedlessness, right? It's a state of heedlessness. Now, another really important thing here is Imam al-Qarafi, Radilah Anu said, gave us, he wrote a book called, all it is is differences in the Sharia.

It tells you the differences between all these different things that appear to be similar. And one of them is the difference between arriba, bilqaba, walmakbibi. So when you're looking at the qaba, you have two things.

You have the qaba itself. You have the qaba itself, and the qaba, and you're gonna get into some difference of opinion about qaba and qada. And you know, there is a difference, but it's very subtle, right? The qaba is that Allah, some say it's one or the other.

The maturidi and the sha'ara differ. They have absolute opposite interpretations. But the point is it's one or the other.

It's not really important which one it is. One of them, the qaba, in this case, is that Allah has decided to make something. And the qada is that he enabled it to happen.

In other words, gave the means, measured out the means in which it could happen. But the qaba is that he qaba'u, qaba'r abukha. He decided to do something.

And the qada is enabling that thing that Allah decided to happen. Now, the makbibi is the thing that happens itself. Now, should we be happy with the makbibi? Should we be happy? Everything that happens? Okay, who said no? Why not? Why shouldn't we be happy with everything that happens? Because bad things happen, right? I mean, don't forget that Allah has, in his qada, is khair and shar.

So you should not be pleased with shar. Because Allah is not pleased with it, right? So Allah has qaba'u, he's decided that there's going to be evil in the world that he created, but he is not pleased with it. In other words, he doesn't reward it.

That's what his rihabah is, his reward. He doesn't reward it. And so when you see malsiya, it's not something that you should like or be pleased with.

Allah doesn't want you to be pleased with it. But you should not be displeased but that Allah has created a world in which that exists. In other words, so you see the difference? One is recognizing that we should not be displeased with Allah Subh'anaHu Wa Ta-A'la and the world that he created.

Because it's Allah's world, he created it. But two, there's things in the world that Allah has placed in there as a challenge to the believers. And that is what you should not be pleased with, those things.

Because he doesn't want you to be pleased with them. Because if you were pleased with them, you wouldn't do anything about changing them. And if you're displeased with them, that becomes the impetus, the compelling reason for you to get out and actually transform what's wrong in the society.

All right? And so that is the difference. Now, an example that what little Khadim gives, actually Imam al-Qarafi gives it, is he says, here's the difference. I think there's a really good analogy.

Just for takribah and ma'ani, that's all. He says, you have a doctor. And you go to the doctor, and the doctor says, you've got gangrene, I have to cut your arm off.

So he cuts your arm off. Now, one day the doctor's out walking by and hears you talking to a friend. And now you've got your arm there, and you're saying, I went to this stupid doctor, I didn't get a second opinion, I know this guy shouldn't have done this.

Now, the doctor hears that, he's going to get upset, right? Because he obviously doesn't want to hurt you. He wants to help you. But in helping you, you had to cut off your arm.

Now, what he's using that analogy for somebody that goes out and says, I don't deserve this. Like, why did I lose all my money? I didn't do anything to deserve this. Why did Allah take away all my money? Now, he said, if, on the other hand, the doctor's walking down and he hears you saying to somebody else, I went to this incredible doctor, and he cut off my arm, and it's causing me so much pain, I can't believe it.

And it was the most severe treatment that I've ever had, and I can't sleep at night. When the doctor hears that, he's going to feel compassion for you. Because you haven't said anything against him.

All you said is this treatment was really intense. And so what he's saying is, if you say about things that Allah does to you in the world, Allah's really doing it to me this time. It's hard, but, Mashallah, there's a wisdom in here.

In other words, that is not going to make Allah angry. It's better not to tell people. If you go and tell other people, it's not, but it doesn't mean that you're not.

You become, you're no longer patient when you start telling people, I don't deserve this. Why is this happening to me? You see, there's a big difference there in the two reactions. One of them is, you are complaining, but you're complaining in a sense you're getting released something, you're releasing something, not complaining about what Allah has done to you, complaining about the difficulty you're having in dealing with it.

That's all. And there's a big difference. So that is an important thing to note.

And he said another thing is that we were not commanded by Allah that we should enjoy difficulties and tribulations. There's nothing in Allah, nowhere in the Quran tells you, enjoy horrible things that happen to you. That's not the way he made us, right? And so you don't have takleef there.

But what Allah Subh'anaHu Wa Ta-A'la says is, and in fact, he actually censures the people in the Quran when he says, We gave them a punishment and it didn't humble them and it didn't make them call on Allah. Please take away this punishment from us. In other words, like now, you see, you look at people now, it just gets worse every year, and people, they just throw up their arms, right? Muslim countries, Kuwait, you look what happened to Kuwait, you would think that they'd all be aliyah now.

They'd all be up every night doing tahajjud, begging Allah, please don't ever let this happen to us again. Things go back. There's no Istikana.

There's no sense of Al-Buddiya to Allah. So tribulations happen, and if they don't change people, then Allah Subh'anaHu Wa Ta-A'la, you know, it's just, it's a bad state to be in. Then he says, the next thing that he says is, Suma'ah.

Suma'ah is one of the diseases of the heart, and suma'ah, the best way to understand suma'ah, is suma'ah is identical to riya, except riya relates to the ni'ma of the ayin, an ayin, and suma'ah to the udon. In other words, suma'ah is that you want other people to hear about you, or you have to tell them about the thing. I did the, oh, you won't believe.

I did the most amazing thing. I gave $10,000 to this and that, you know, charity, or whatever. What you're doing is you want to tell people about good actions that you did, and that's called suma'ah.

And the Prophet SallAllahu Alaihi Wasallam said, man samma'ah, samma'Allahu alahi wa yamaqiyana. Whoever does this suma'ah tells people all the things they did. Allah is going to tell people what they really did on the yomuqiyama.

You see? Because everybody has the skeletons, right? Everybody has those skeletons. Kulu kum kappa'on. All of you do wrong.

Ukhirul kappa'ina tawaboon. So everybody has akhpa. And so if you're going around telling people what good you're doing, Allah says he's going to tell them what bad you were doing as well on yomuqiyama, and you don't want that, so don't say anything.

So he says, was suma'atul ikbar of the pa'ati, ba'ad al-ghulusihah min al-athati. So you did the action for Allah, but then you went and told people about it. And so, akhbat al-amad, you ruin it.

Libad al-aradi ar-riya i-wal-amad tufsidhu, what I can intubtam damad. So what he's saying is, you know, that there was riya related to it, and it ruins action. Suma'at ruins your action.

But if you make toba, then it rectifies it. All right? So you can make toba from that. And he said also the one who does it in order to tell other people about it.

So he didn't do it with yikhlas. He actually did it in the first place in order to tell other people, and that's also suma'at. That's also.

And in the note there, the difference between seeking reputation by telling others of one's deeds and showing off is that the former involves a sense of hearing and the latter of seeing. In other words, they are one but use different routes to affect the hearts of others. Ibn Abd al-Saram says that should the reason be a desire to encourage others or to relate Allah's blessings upon you, then I mean it.

The hadith says whoever informs others of his good deeds out of desire for their benefit. That's not in the hadith, but that's the explanation. Allah will inform them of his foul deeds on the day of judgment.

And whoever dislikes his good deeds, displays his good deeds to others. Allah will display his bad deeds to them on the yomukhayam. It's related by Ahmad and Imam Muslim.

They always put one of the sunnah of the muhadithin is to put Imam Ahmad first because of his... Even though Sahih Muslim is more sound than the Muslim of Imam Ahmad, the Jalala of Imam Ahmad's maqam in the ummah, his exalted place, puts him first before there. So you'll see that very often in books that he's mentioned before Imam Muslim. And now he says he's going to tell us what's going on.

This is very interesting here. He was a brigand from Beni Ababa. And so the Arabs, they use him as aqtam min shilab.

He's like a proverb. If you want to say somebody's a really bad robber, then they say aqtam min shilab. You know, he's more of a brigand than even shilab.

So he's saying, this brigand that that means like a highway robber, severs the road. On all those people trying to get to their Lord, this brigand that's going to stop you on the road and try to rob you of your rewards with Allah is tama, covetousness. So we go back to this source disease of desire, right? The Arab, Abra Abbas al-Mursi said one day, He said, Abra Abbas al-Mursi was once in the marketplace and he was going to buy something and he saw somebody he knew.

And he was a very righteous man. He was known for scholarship and his spirituality. And so he thought, if I go to that person, he'll say, oh, I'll give you a deal because he's a religious person.

And he said, he heard a call, nuditu. He got a call like a hadtif. And the hadtif told him that a salam al-fideen, to be sound in your deen, is not to have desire for created things.

So that is how you have a sound deen, is to lose that tama in other people no matter what. And that's what he's warning us against because, again, if you look at these previous diseases, all these things, you think you're going to get something from them. Right? You think that you're going to get something from them.

And the pama, the reason for pama, according to Sidi Ahmad Zaroq, radiAllahu alayhi wa sallam, is a ghafla. You forget that you've become heedless that Allah is nazi and dar. Human beings don't have anything to benefit you.

So there's a ghafla that comes on the heart and Allah Subh'anaHu Wa Ta-A'la warns us about that very often in the Qur'an. And one of the verses is a reminder in Allahu wa razaq. Allah He is the razaq.

He's the one that provides for you. Another one, Allah Subh'anaHu Wa Ta-A'la says, that Inyam siska, Inyam siska Allahu bi adurren, fadaa kashiva lahu. Lahu wa Subh'anaHu Wa Ta-A'la.

If Allah gives you a harm, there's nobody that can remove that harm except Allah Subh'anaHu Wa Ta-A'la. In the famous hadith when the Prophet SallAllahu Alaihi Wasallam saw Ibn Abbas, he said, yeah, hudam. He called Ibn Abbas, he said, I'm going to teach you some words.

Amongst what he taught him was that if all of the creation got together to harm you and Allah didn't decree it for you, they couldn't do it. And if all of creation got together to prevent some harm getting to you, they couldn't prevent it. It's over.

The ink has been lifted, the pens are dry. The pens have been lifted, the ink is dry. It's over.

So everything that's going to happen to you, you have to recognize, is in the hands of Allah Subh'anaHu Wa Ta-A'la. And then you should desire Allah Subh'anaHu Wa Ta-A'la, not what people have. And then, like in an example, people wish you ill how though? When they say bad things against you? Well, if you're straight with Allah, then their prayer is going to be of no benefit.

If you're oppressing them, then you should have fear, because the Prophet SallAllahu Alaihi Wasallam said, fear the prayer of the oppressor. If you're not an oppressor, you don't have any fear of their prayer. If you're not oppressing them or anybody else, then you shouldn't have any fear, because somebody who's making a prayer against you, that's voting against you.

And it'll just go back to them. It doesn't go back to you, it'll go back to them. And that's their business.

Somebody making a prayer against you, you know. That's their business. You know, Allah didn't tell us to make prayers against people, unless they're oppressors.

The truthful merchant is with the martyrs on the yomukhayama, the hadith. The truthful merchant is with the martyrs, which means he doesn't have thama in his heart. Now, how do you say, how can you be a merchant and not have thama? Because he sees everything coming from Allah.

If Allah brings him a customer, it's Allah bringing his provision to him. If he doesn't buy anything, Allah didn't decree it. And he can just be a human being.

And what's been allotted to us in this life is called a risk. And there's some really important concepts here. One of them is, rarely does he use two names with the same attribute.

You'll find it, like rafir al-foor, things like that. He's a razaq and he's a razaq. And we're called marzuk.

We are marzuk. We're the ones, the passive form. And what we're getting is called risk.

And I mentioned last week, there's a difference of opinion about risk. Some say it's what you actually benefit from and others say it's whatever you have. Dominant opinion is what you benefit.

There's nothing on the earth except its risk is from Allah. Allah has given it its risk. because the nature of the world is that Allah has divided the provision of people, the arzaq of people.

And there's two types of arzaq. There's risk which is bopani and there's risk which is lahiri. Those are the two types.

The rizq that is outward is your food, your health, those type of things that we can see. The bopani rizq is things like your company, your knowledge, the things that you actually learn, the character that you have, all of these type things. Now, if you look, one of the interesting things about life is the fact that the same people keep showing up in your life.

This is a very strange phenomenon about life. When you realize that there's only there's five billion people on the planet and yet if you actually think of the main people that are important in your life, they're actually a very small number and they keep coming back again and again and again because they're actually part of your rizq. They're in your rizq.

So your company is going to be, you'll keep seeing the same people again and again. Now, and there's tribulation and benefit. Some of it's good, some of it's bad.

Some people have very pessimistic view, other people optimistic, especially if you realize it's all from Allah. Allah is the one doing it. Now, here's what's really important to remember.

Because Allah has given the human being wealth and his wealth is a nirmah and he placed it in you and then in his divine decree he has distributed the arzah, these provisions amongst people. But some of the risk of one person, he put with another person and there's a sabab that means you have to go and get that. Do you see? So there's this thing, here's the person over there, he's got your rizq.

It's not his rizq, it's your rizq. But Allah put it in his possession. Now, however you get it, it depends.

If it's food that he has that was written for you, he'll invite you over for dinner. He might not even like you, but if it was written for you, you have to come to dinner. Maybe you weren't invited, you show up with so-and-so.

Right? And that's rizq, it's there and it's makson and he can't eat it because it's not for him, it's for you. And that's why the Prophet SallAllahu Alaihi Wasallam said he loved the guests more than anything. He came with his own provision.

You're not losing anything by feeding the guests. It's his provision, it wasn't written for you, it's for him. And he leaves with the wrong actions of the house.

In other words, it's a kafara when you're kind to a guest and courteous, Allah will remove wrong action. So the guest is a great blessing from Allah. Now, because the asbab, you have these asbab and they're very mysterious, people start thinking, my rizq is from him.

That's the kafla. Because Allah has made the sunnah where he's put your rizq with somebody else. And so people start seeing, he benefits those guys.

Maybe I can get something from him too. And so that's where the famah comes in, where you start seeing the sabab and lose sight of the musabdib. You see the means, but you lose sight of the true means, the true musabdib, the cause of those means.

And this is what happens, and this is where the famah comes in and this is what's so dangerous. And the biggest sign of this is when you get angry when you don't get what you want. That's the biggest sign.

When there's an expectation, it's not fulfilled, you become angry. Now, this is obviously not about hukuk. In other words, if somebody owes you something and he's not giving you it and he has it, that's a vulnerable.

And you get angry because it's vulnerable. This is talking about arzaq and what's coming to you and what's yours. And this is why the Prophet SAW said in the hadith, he said that the rizq is coming to you.

He said, Allahumma siri Anasina Muhammad, that Jibril said to him, no soul will die until it completes the rizq that was allotted to it. It has to complete it. It's amazing.

He died. And then even more extraordinary, it's not that extraordinary in Mauritania. The man who was meeting us at the plane was good friends with him.

So we learned his whole story. He was like a minister and very well respected person. And he'd become ill.

He was only about 44. And he was in Nirma. And he was out in the desert recuperating.

And he took a shot. Somebody gave him a shot of something. He went into a coma.

They took him anyway. They were bringing him to Noakshap. The point is he had an appointment with the Angel of Death at that place.

There he was out in the middle of the desert in Mauritania in a tent. But he was meant to die in Noakshap. So all the as-bab were made so that he could die where he was decreed to die.

Because there was no other place he could die. It was his, it was Maksun for him to be in that place at that time and to die there. And so, you know, if Allah, you know, If Allah wants to destroy somebody in a certain place, He'll give him reasons to go to that place.

And it's an amazing thing. So the same is true about risk. And this is why the Prophet SallAllahu Alaihi Wasallam said that, Jibril is telling us all your risk is written for you.

And then he said, So have takwa of Allah. Don't do anything haram to get your risk because it's coming to you. And don't be somebody who's going and begging or asking people.

And that was one of the things that the Sahaba took bayah with the messenger of Allah. One of the things of bayah was that they didn't ask people for money. That was actually in the bayah that you did not ask people for money.

So, you know, the iman should free you from all those concerns. And then he said something, He said, don't let the delay of your risk cause you to be disobedient to Allah. In other words, if it's not coming as soon as you want it to come, don't let that be a cause for you becoming disobedient with Allah.

So that's really important. Everybody in Salah. The next disease is extended hope.

Salah will cover that next week. We last week we covered which was covetous desire. And then what he says in there, what he says is He says that the Arabs have a, they call it sumu sa'a, which is a poison that acts immediately.

Quick acting, immediate acting poison. The immediate acting poison of this love of dunya and pama and all the diseases of the heart is tatwil al amal. And then he gives us a definition of it, which is accustoming or assuring yourself that death is a long way off.

And this is something obviously, on the one hand, it's a necessary human condition. And there's an amazing wisdom in tatwil al amal. And one of the wisdoms that the ulama have mentioned about tatwil al amal is that if people did not have extended hope, they wouldn't bother planting trees.

And if you thought you were going to die any minute, you're not really going to be worrying about farming or having children. And so what would happen is there'd be no infrastructure for the next generation. Every generation would have to come and deal with the immediate circumstances.

But instead, because human beings do have hopes that they're going to live, they build things, they farm, they create infrastructure, and that infrastructure is inherited by the next generation. And the famous story in one of the Persian stories is about the Persian shah who passed by an old man planting an olive tree. And the king said, You know, is this tree going to be of any benefit for you? Because you'll die surely before it comes to fruition, because a good olive tree takes several decades before it really is producing good olives.

And so the sheikh said, and it became a proverb amongst the Arabs, They planted before us, and we ate from what they planted. And so we plant so that those coming after us will eat. So the king gave him a reward.

And then he said, already the tree benefited me. And so he gave him more. So the idea of this tatwil al amal is that it's a necessary, it's a rahma from Allah, that Allah has put this in, because people wouldn't go to school, people wouldn't do anything.

Now, one of the things about modern society is because of the apocalyptic nature of the age we're living in, people are beginning to lose hope about the future. A lot of young people are becoming very nihilistic, dressing in black, right, at these schools and things like this. There's a morbid perspective on the world.

And the fast food culture, which is the idea of, you know, get it now, don't wait. And the credit culture is a culture that really is causing people to lose this sense that there is a future. And in fact, it's only recently, this is the first generation of Americans that actually see that their future will be worse than their parents.

Prior to that, I mean, Americans were noted for their cheery optimism about the future. And they're losing that, right? And so it's actually a bad sign when people do lose hope. On the other hand, what he's saying is that it's also a deadly poison if you have too much hope.

It becomes a false hope, an aman, ul al aman. And the result of it is that it generates hard-heartedness and indolence. So he says, yul hitu kaswatir kurubi wal kasal.

What that means is by ul al aman, the heart becomes hard because it doesn't reflect on akhirah. It doesn't reflect on its own mortality, on its own dependence. And so it gets a hard heart.

Now, Al-Fubayl ibn Ayyad, radiAllahu Anhu said, and he's one of the great early scholars and zuhad, the people of asceticism or of doing without. He said, chamsul min alamt al-shakha. There's five things that are signs of shakha, which is wretchedness.

The world is divided into two types of people, saeed and shakhi. There's only two types. That's it. 

No third. Saeed and shakhi. Saeed is felicitous, and shakhi is wretched.

And the idea is obviously... Now, if you look at Aristotle, who's kind of foundational to Western civilization, Aristotle wrote a book called The Ethics. And in this book, he basically says the goal in life is leisure and happiness. And this is reflected in the Declaration of Independence, because the Declaration of Independence, written by Thomas Jefferson, he says in there that people have these inalienable rights from God given to them.

And he says, among which are life, haiya, hakul haiya, liberty, hariya. These are sharia rights as well. But then he says, and the pursuit of happiness.

And his original wording, he actually said, the right to be free of debt when you come into the world. Because what happens now is that because of national debt, children are born in debt. I think it's about $50,000 in debt.

Every child that's born is automatically in debt based on their previous generations overspending. So that was the original phrase. But then he changed it to the pursuit of happiness.

Now, what is happiness to a middle-aged white landowner in 1776 having more land? So their idea of happiness really was the pursuit of stuff, of goods. And this is part and parcel of the modern American dream. Happiness is seen as pursuing stuff.

What Islam did is it radically altered human beings' understanding of what happiness is. Happiness is not necessarily a quote-unquote good life. According to the Muslims, happiness is sa'ada in the next world.

If you're sa'id in the next world, it doesn't matter how you lived your life in this world. And the previous class, when we were looking at the sira, the du'a of the Prophet SallAllahu Alaihi Wasallam, when he said, if you're not angry with me, then I don't care what you do with me. In other words, if my life is miserable in this world, but I haven't incurred your wrath, I'm happy with that.

Because he knows that the akhirah, chayru lakamine l'ulah, the end world, the afterlife, is better than the first life. So anyway, the opposite concept of shakawah is sa'ada, felicitousness, is shakawah. Shakawah is the idea of, in the next world, the person doesn't have akhirah.

They're shakhi. That's what it means. So Fubayr ibn Ayyab said that there were five signs of shakawah.

The first one was kaswat al-khali, a hard heart. That's a sign of a person's shakhi. And the Prophet SallAllahu Alaihi Wasallam said to a man who saw him kiss a baby, he said, I kissed my children.

I have ten children, I never kissed one from them. And that's the hilva, you know, it's a pride in being a tough Arab. And the Prophet SallAllahu Alaihi Wasallam said, I don't have anything in my deen for a man who Allah has removed rahma from his heart.

So hard heart, it means that you don't have a portion of the deen, right? Have mercy on those in the earth and he in the heavens will have mercy on you. So kaswat al-khali, the eye that doesn't weep. And the ulama say, if you want to know whether your heart is hard, then look at your eye.

Because if the eye is dry, it's an indication of a hard heart, right? So the person who has riqah in the heart has a wet eye and the person who doesn't has a hard heart. And the next is qillatul hayah, lacking modesty. People that don't have shame.

If you have no shame, do whatever you want. If you don't have shame, do what you want. So qillatul hayah is a sign of shakkawah.

Ar-rahvatuf id dunya, desiring dunya, is a sign of shakkawah. Because they say that no one increases in his akhirah except that it harms his dunya. And no one increases in his dunya except that it harms his akhirah.

By the nature, because they're two opposites. So increase in one is decrease in the other. Now this does not mean wealthy people automatically you assume that, oh.
